Construction and Architecture English

A construction and architecture English curriculum for design intent, RFIs, change orders, site coordination, safety, schedule pressure, permitting, punch lists, claims, and client communication.

For architects, construction managers, project engineers, site supervisors, estimators, owners' representatives, subcontractor coordinators, and design-build teams.

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Turn broad client language into specific functional requirements without claiming design approval.
  • Describe a document discrepancy and request a traceable decision without inventing precedence.
  • Distinguish pricing, approval, and authorization while explaining a proposed change in cost and time.
  • Explain a constrained sequence and distinguish forecast movement from contractual entitlement.
  • Request task-specific, approved safety information and confirm understanding without inventing a work method.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

01Design Intent and Client RequirementsWhat does a flexible meeting room need to do?+

Your goal

Turn broad client language into specific functional requirements without claiming design approval.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

What does a flexible meeting room need to do?

Client representative Amira asks architect Ben for a flexible meeting room. During briefing, she specifies workshops for 12 people at movable tables and presentations for 24 people in rows, at different times. Staff should be able to change the arrangement within 15 minutes. Remote participants must hear questions from anywhere in the room. These are requested performance targets, not verified design capabilities or approved occupant loads. Ben will prepare two layouts and seek audiovisual and code reviews. The room dimensions, storage provision, access requirements, and budget effects have not yet been assessed.

Amira: Client representative / Ben: Architect

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which use pattern does Amira request?

Answer and explanation

C. Two arrangements used at different times

The brief distinguishes a twelve-person workshop from a separate twenty-four-person presentation.

2. What does the 15-minute figure describe?

Answer and explanation

D. A requested furniture-change target

Fifteen minutes is the requested changeover target, not a tested or approved performance result.

3. Which statement about capacity is supported?

Answer and explanation

C. The requested numbers still need design and code review.

Requested user numbers do not establish a compliant layout or an approved occupant load.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Requested capacity is not approved occupant load. A layout drawing must still be assessed against the actual space and applicable requirements. Keep the client wish, proposed solution, and verified result distinct.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
design intent
The purpose and performance an intended design should achieve.explain the design intent
client brief
A statement of the client's needs, priorities, and constraints.develop the client brief
functional requirement
A description of what a space or system must enable.define a functional requirement
space planning
Organizing space for activities, movement, and equipment.review the space planning
occupant load
The number of occupants determined under applicable code provisions.verify the occupant load
seating arrangement
The planned position and relationship of seats.compare seating arrangements
theater-style seating
Rows of seats generally facing a presentation area.propose theater-style seating
workshop layout
An arrangement supporting collaborative or practical group work.develop a workshop layout
reconfigurable furniture
Furniture that can be rearranged for different uses.specify reconfigurable furniture
changeover time
The time needed to change between arrangements or uses.set a changeover target
circulation
Routes and space for movement within a building.maintain clear circulation
egress
Movement out of a building or space, particularly for emergency exit.review egress requirements
accessibility
Usability of spaces and facilities by people with differing abilities.integrate accessibility requirements
sightline
A line of vision from a viewing position to an object or area.check presentation sightlines
acoustic performance
How a space handles sound for its intended use.assess acoustic performance
reverberation
Persistence of sound after the original source stops.evaluate reverberation
speech intelligibility
How clearly spoken words can be understood.improve speech intelligibility
audiovisual (AV) system
Equipment for presenting or transmitting sound and images.coordinate the AV system
microphone coverage
The area from which a microphone system can capture usable sound.assess microphone coverage
power provision
The planned availability and location of electrical power.coordinate power provision
storage allowance
Space reserved for items that are not currently in use.include a storage allowance
adjacency
The relationship between nearby spaces or functions.test an adjacency requirement
performance target
A desired measurable result that must still be evaluated.state a performance target
design constraint
A condition limiting the available design choices.identify a design constraint

A room may support two arrangements without supporting both simultaneously. Name the use pattern and changeover target. For remote participation, sound quality needs its own requirement rather than an assumption that a screen solves the problem.

C Notice the language

Clarification is part of professional service

A client may expect the designer to infer a complete solution from a familiar adjective. Ask about observable uses before recommending a form. Restating the client request accurately shows attention while leaving feasibility and regulatory review to the appropriate process.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Clarify a broad adjective
When you say flexible, which activities do you need?
Separate use patterns
Will those activities happen at the same time?
Quantify workshop needs
The workshop arrangement needs twelve places at movable tables.
Quantify presentation needs
The presentation arrangement needs twenty-four seats in rows.
State the changeover target
Staff should be able to change layouts within fifteen minutes.
Qualify the target
That is a requested target, not a tested capability.
Ask about storage
Where will unused furniture be stored?
Specify the listening need
Remote participants need to hear questions throughout the room.
Separate AV functions
Displaying slides does not by itself provide microphone coverage.
Check sightlines
We need to assess views from the proposed seating positions.
Name the review boundary
The layouts still need access and code review.
Avoid a capacity promise
I cannot confirm the approved occupant load from this brief.
Preserve a constraint
We have not yet assessed the budget effect.
Offer a defined next step
I will prepare two layouts for review.
Confirm the brief
The two activities occur at different times.
Close the meeting
I will record these as requirements to be tested.
6 language and precision notes
Needs versus has
A required capability is not an existing verified capability.
At different times
Excludes simultaneous use from the supplied requirement.
Within
Gives a maximum target duration, not an approximate starting time.
Can versus should
Can may claim ability; should here expresses a requested target.
Throughout
Extends the listening requirement beyond the presenter position.
For review
Keeps a proposed layout distinct from an approved design.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which brief statement is most precise?

Answer and explanation

B. Twelve-person workshops and twenty-four-person presentations at different times

The precise statement identifies both user numbers and the separate timing of the activities.

2. Which claim goes beyond the facts?

Answer and explanation

A. The room is already approved for twenty-four occupants.

The client request does not establish an approved occupant load or compliant design.

3. Which question clarifies the changeover requirement?

Answer and explanation

C. Who moves the furniture, and where does unused furniture go?

Staffing and storage directly affect the fifteen-minute changeover; the other questions address different design needs.

4. Which response addresses remote participants?

Answer and explanation

A. Assess sound capture from the room, not just the display.

Hearing questions depends on the audio arrangement, not simply the presence of a screen.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

What does a flexible meeting room need to do?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Amira: Client representative / Ben: Architect

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • microphone coverage
  • changeover time
  • client brief
  • circulation
  • sightlines
  • seating arrangements
  • design constraints
  • occupant load
  • functional requirements
  • speech intelligibility
  1. Amira
    We need a flexible meeting room. At the moment, every session feels squeezed into the same arrangement, even when the activity changes completely.
  2. Ben
    Let's make the 1 more specific. Are you describing different arrangements at different times, or two groups using separate parts of the room simultaneously?
  3. Amira
    Different times. We run workshops for twelve people at movable tables, then presentations for twenty-four people in rows. We do not need both together.
  4. Ben
    That gives us two distinct 2. I will draw both, but those numbers remain requested capacities until the layouts and relevant requirements have been reviewed.
  5. Amira
    Good. Staff also need to switch between them quickly. I would like the room ready for the next session within fifteen minutes, without specialist assistance.
  6. Ben
    I will record that 3 as a performance target. We still need to assess the furniture, available storage, and who will actually make the change.
  7. Amira
    Could we simply leave the spare tables against the wall? That seems easier than creating a separate storage area somewhere else in the building.
  8. Ben
    We need to check the 4 and access implications before choosing that solution. Unused furniture still occupies space, and the room dimensions have not yet been assessed.
  9. Amira
    Understood. The presentation layout must also work for people joining remotely. They often hear the presenter but miss questions from people sitting further back.
  10. Ben
    That is a 5 requirement, not just a request for a larger screen. I will ask the AV specialist to assess sound capture across the room.
  11. Amira
    That is our current problem: a good screen, but remote colleagues cannot hear questions from the back. Please include those seats in the audio review.
  12. Ben
    We should also consider 6. Capturing sound does not guarantee that words are clear. The room's acoustic behavior will need professional assessment alongside the equipment.
  13. Amira
    Can I tell our team the new room will hold twenty-four people? They want to start scheduling larger presentations as soon as the design begins.
  14. Ben
    Say that twenty-four is the requested presentation capacity. I cannot confirm the 7 from this brief or authorize use before the required reviews.
  15. Amira
    I will tell the team the numbers are still being tested. Please also check whether people at the sides can see the diagrams.
  16. Ben
    I will include 8 in the layout review. That requirement may affect furniture placement and display location, so we should not fix either independently too early.
  17. Amira
    What will you bring back to our next meeting? I need something concrete enough for staff to discuss without assuming the design is already settled.
  18. Ben
    Two proposed layouts and an explicit list of 9. I will identify the outstanding dimensional, storage, access, AV, code, and budget reviews beside the requested performance.
  19. Amira
    That works. Please preserve the twelve and twenty-four figures, different-time use, and fifteen-minute target, while clearly showing that feasibility remains to be established.
  20. Ben
    Agreed. Those will form the recorded 10. The next step is to test them against the actual room and project constraints, not to label them approved capabilities.

Answers and explanations
  1. client brief Turn 2. The client brief records the intended users, activities, and requirements before a specific solution is confirmed.
  2. seating arrangements Turn 4. The seating arrangements describe how seats relate to each activity; they do not establish approved occupancy or feasibility.
  3. changeover time Turn 6. Changeover time is the duration needed to move between the requested arrangements, here a target of fifteen minutes.
  4. circulation Turn 8. Circulation concerns movement through the space; stored furniture must not be assumed to fit without assessing its effect on routes.
  5. microphone coverage Turn 10. Microphone coverage addresses capturing sound from the required locations, rather than merely displaying images or slides.
  6. speech intelligibility Turn 12. Speech intelligibility describes how clearly the spoken words can be understood, beyond the mere presence of an audio signal.
  7. occupant load Turn 14. Occupant load requires the applicable code determination; the requested number of seats is not that approval.
  8. sightlines Turn 16. Sightlines describe the views from seating positions to the presentation area and must be assessed in the proposed layouts.
  9. design constraints Turn 18. Design constraints are conditions limiting the solution; identifying them prevents unresolved matters from being treated as settled design decisions.
  10. functional requirements Turn 20. Functional requirements state what the space is intended to enable, while the design and review process establishes how that can be achieved.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Amira: We need a flexible meeting room. At the moment, every session feels squeezed into the same arrangement, even when the activity changes completely.
  2. Ben: Let's make the client brief more specific. Are you describing different arrangements at different times, or two groups using separate parts of the room simultaneously?
  3. Amira: Different times. We run workshops for twelve people at movable tables, then presentations for twenty-four people in rows. We do not need both together.
  4. Ben: That gives us two distinct seating arrangements. I will draw both, but those numbers remain requested capacities until the layouts and relevant requirements have been reviewed.
  5. Amira: Good. Staff also need to switch between them quickly. I would like the room ready for the next session within fifteen minutes, without specialist assistance.
  6. Ben: I will record that changeover time as a performance target. We still need to assess the furniture, available storage, and who will actually make the change.
  7. Amira: Could we simply leave the spare tables against the wall? That seems easier than creating a separate storage area somewhere else in the building.
  8. Ben: We need to check the circulation and access implications before choosing that solution. Unused furniture still occupies space, and the room dimensions have not yet been assessed.
  9. Amira: Understood. The presentation layout must also work for people joining remotely. They often hear the presenter but miss questions from people sitting further back.
  10. Ben: That is a microphone coverage requirement, not just a request for a larger screen. I will ask the AV specialist to assess sound capture across the room.
  11. Amira: That is our current problem: a good screen, but remote colleagues cannot hear questions from the back. Please include those seats in the audio review.
  12. Ben: We should also consider speech intelligibility. Capturing sound does not guarantee that words are clear. The room's acoustic behavior will need professional assessment alongside the equipment.
  13. Amira: Can I tell our team the new room will hold twenty-four people? They want to start scheduling larger presentations as soon as the design begins.
  14. Ben: Say that twenty-four is the requested presentation capacity. I cannot confirm the occupant load from this brief or authorize use before the required reviews.
  15. Amira: I will tell the team the numbers are still being tested. Please also check whether people at the sides can see the diagrams.
  16. Ben: I will include sightlines in the layout review. That requirement may affect furniture placement and display location, so we should not fix either independently too early.
  17. Amira: What will you bring back to our next meeting? I need something concrete enough for staff to discuss without assuming the design is already settled.
  18. Ben: Two proposed layouts and an explicit list of design constraints. I will identify the outstanding dimensional, storage, access, AV, code, and budget reviews beside the requested performance.
  19. Amira: That works. Please preserve the twelve and twenty-four figures, different-time use, and fifteen-minute target, while clearly showing that feasibility remains to be established.
  20. Ben: Agreed. Those will form the recorded functional requirements. The next step is to test them against the actual room and project constraints, not to label them approved capabilities.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Flexible for which activities?

Fictional design briefing: a client requests a flexible meeting room but has not specified occupancy, furniture changes, or audiovisual requirements.

  1. Architect: Your brief calls the meeting room flexible. Which activities should that flexibility support?
  2. Client facilities manager: We want presentations and small-group discussions, but haven't agreed attendance numbers for either.
  3. Architect: Could you confirm who can define the occupancy requirements before we develop the room program?
  4. Client facilities manager: Our events lead can do that. I shouldn't give you a number based on my guess.
  5. Architect: Thank you. Do you expect staff to move furniture between sessions, or keep one arrangement?
  6. Client facilities manager: That is still undecided. Would those choices affect the design intent and equipment requirements?
  7. Architect: Yes. They affect what we need to investigate, including furniture, audiovisual needs, and constructability.
  8. Client facilities manager: Then let's separate the known activities from the unresolved operational requirements in the scope.
  9. Architect: Agreed. Please ask the events lead about attendance and changeovers; I'll gather the audiovisual questions.
  10. Client facilities manager: I'll return with confirmed requirements rather than treating flexible as a complete specification.
Accessible to whom, for what use?

Separate fictional early-design case: a museum client requests an accessible staff workshop; detailed user requirements and specialist review are not yet available.

  1. Project architect: The program says accessible workshop. Could you clarify the activities and users we should plan for?
  2. Museum operations manager: Staff will prepare exhibits there, but we haven't collected their equipment or access requirements.
  3. Project architect: Then we should gather those requirements before assuming a particular layout meets everyone's needs.
  4. Museum operations manager: Can we just copy the workshop at our other site and call that compliant?
  5. Project architect: A copied layout wouldn't establish compliance or suitability here. Those questions need project-specific qualified review.
  6. Museum operations manager: What information should I request from the exhibit team first to clarify the scope?
  7. Project architect: Ask about tasks, equipment, movement, and stated access needs, using the appropriate consultation process.
  8. Museum operations manager: I'll avoid inferring what individuals need from their job titles or appearances.
  9. Project architect: Good. We'll use the confirmed requirements to develop design intent and identify remaining specialist questions.
  10. Museum operations manager: I'll arrange that discussion before presenting the copied layout as an agreed solution.
Open plan does not mean no storage

Separate fictional office brief: a client wants an open-plan reception area; storage quantity and privacy needs remain unspecified.

  1. Interior architect: When you say open plan, do you mean clear sightlines or removing every enclosed space?
  2. Client project manager: Mainly clear sightlines from reception. We still need storage, but haven't agreed how much.
  3. Interior architect: That's an important distinction. What materials will be stored, and who needs access during opening hours?
  4. Client project manager: The reception team holds supplies and visitor materials. I need to confirm volumes with them.
  5. Interior architect: Could you also check whether any conversations need privacy near the reception desk?
  6. Client project manager: Yes. I had assumed open plan meant privacy wasn't part of the scope.
  7. Interior architect: We shouldn't assume that. The room program can distinguish visual openness from functional privacy requirements.
  8. Client project manager: I'll ask the team about both before approving the concept's design intent.
  9. Interior architect: Then I'll develop options against those confirmed needs, rather than simply removing the enclosed storage.
  10. Client project manager: Agreed. Our next discussion will clarify the requirements before we commit to a layout.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

What does a flexible meeting room need to do?
  1. Read the corrected conversation, distinguishing 12 workshop places from 24 presentation seats.
  2. Switch roles and repeat turns 5-12; emphasize the 15-minute target, storage, and hearing questions from the whole room.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

A second room brief

A separate client requests workshops for 10 people and presentations for 18, at different times. The desired changeover is 20 minutes. None of the capacities is approved.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Client: "The workshop needs ___ places."

Answer and explanation

A. 10

Ten is the requested workshop capacity in the supplied brief.

2. Architect: "The presentation request is ___ seats."

Answer and explanation

A. 18

Eighteen refers to presentation seating, not the workshop arrangement.

3. Client: "The target changeover is ___ minutes."

Answer and explanation

C. 20

Twenty minutes is the requested duration for changing arrangements.

4. Architect: "Those capacities remain ___, not approved."

Answer and explanation

A. requested

The brief explicitly says no capacity approval has been established.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Copy a finished AI prompt for this lesson

These prompts are fully written and include this lesson's facts and language. Open one, copy it as written, and paste it into a new AI chat.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Design Intent and Client Requirements
Original fictional case: Client representative Amira asks architect Ben for a flexible meeting room. During briefing, she specifies workshops for 12 people at movable tables and presentations for 24 people in rows, at different times. Staff should be able to change the arrangement within 15 minutes. Remote participants must hear questions from anywhere in the room. These are requested performance targets, not verified design capabilities or approved occupant loads. Ben will prepare two layouts and seek audiovisual and code reviews. The room dimensions, storage provision, access requirements, and budget effects have not yet been assessed.
Language workshop: What does a flexible meeting room need to do?
Communication goal: Turn broad client language into specific functional requirements without claiming design approval.
Language guidance: Requested capacity is not approved occupant load. A layout drawing must still be assessed against the actual space and applicable requirements. Keep the client wish, proposed solution, and verified result distinct.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say flexible, which activities do you need? / Will those activities happen at the same time? / The workshop arrangement needs twelve places at movable tables. / The presentation arrangement needs twenty-four seats in rows. / Staff should be able to change layouts within fifteen minutes. / That is a requested target, not a tested capability. / Where will unused furniture be stored? / Remote participants need to hear questions throughout the room. / Displaying slides does not by itself provide microphone coverage. / We need to assess views from the proposed seating positions. / The layouts still need access and code review. / I cannot confirm the approved occupant load from this brief. / We have not yet assessed the budget effect. / I will prepare two layouts for review. / The two activities occur at different times. / I will record these as requirements to be tested.
Vocabulary: design intent: The purpose and performance an intended design should achieve. / client brief: A statement of the client's needs, priorities, and constraints. / functional requirement: A description of what a space or system must enable. / space planning: Organizing space for activities, movement, and equipment. / occupant load: The number of occupants determined under applicable code provisions. / seating arrangement: The planned position and relationship of seats. / theater-style seating: Rows of seats generally facing a presentation area. / workshop layout: An arrangement supporting collaborative or practical group work. / reconfigurable furniture: Furniture that can be rearranged for different uses. / changeover time: The time needed to change between arrangements or uses. / circulation: Routes and space for movement within a building. / egress: Movement out of a building or space, particularly for emergency exit. / accessibility: Usability of spaces and facilities by people with differing abilities. / sightline: A line of vision from a viewing position to an object or area. / acoustic performance: How a space handles sound for its intended use. / reverberation: Persistence of sound after the original source stops. / speech intelligibility: How clearly spoken words can be understood. / audiovisual (AV) system: Equipment for presenting or transmitting sound and images. / microphone coverage: The area from which a microphone system can capture usable sound. / power provision: The planned availability and location of electrical power. / storage allowance: Space reserved for items that are not currently in use. / adjacency: The relationship between nearby spaces or functions. / performance target: A desired measurable result that must still be evaluated. / design constraint: A condition limiting the available design choices.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation, distinguishing 12 workshop places from 24 presentation seats. Switch roles and repeat turns 5-12; emphasize the 15-minute target, storage, and hearing questions from the whole room. Read the checked transfer twice using 10, 18, and 20. Keep requested capacity distinct from approved occupancy.
Partner: Amira: Client representative / Ben: Architect

Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Design Intent and Client Requirements
Original fictional case: Client representative Amira asks architect Ben for a flexible meeting room. During briefing, she specifies workshops for 12 people at movable tables and presentations for 24 people in rows, at different times. Staff should be able to change the arrangement within 15 minutes. Remote participants must hear questions from anywhere in the room. These are requested performance targets, not verified design capabilities or approved occupant loads. Ben will prepare two layouts and seek audiovisual and code reviews. The room dimensions, storage provision, access requirements, and budget effects have not yet been assessed.
Language workshop: What does a flexible meeting room need to do?
Communication goal: Turn broad client language into specific functional requirements without claiming design approval.
Language guidance: Requested capacity is not approved occupant load. A layout drawing must still be assessed against the actual space and applicable requirements. Keep the client wish, proposed solution, and verified result distinct.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): When you say flexible, which activities do you need? / Will those activities happen at the same time? / The workshop arrangement needs twelve places at movable tables. / The presentation arrangement needs twenty-four seats in rows. / Staff should be able to change layouts within fifteen minutes. / That is a requested target, not a tested capability. / Where will unused furniture be stored? / Remote participants need to hear questions throughout the room. / Displaying slides does not by itself provide microphone coverage. / We need to assess views from the proposed seating positions. / The layouts still need access and code review. / I cannot confirm the approved occupant load from this brief. / We have not yet assessed the budget effect. / I will prepare two layouts for review. / The two activities occur at different times. / I will record these as requirements to be tested.
Vocabulary: design intent: The purpose and performance an intended design should achieve. / client brief: A statement of the client's needs, priorities, and constraints. / functional requirement: A description of what a space or system must enable. / space planning: Organizing space for activities, movement, and equipment. / occupant load: The number of occupants determined under applicable code provisions. / seating arrangement: The planned position and relationship of seats. / theater-style seating: Rows of seats generally facing a presentation area. / workshop layout: An arrangement supporting collaborative or practical group work. / reconfigurable furniture: Furniture that can be rearranged for different uses. / changeover time: The time needed to change between arrangements or uses. / circulation: Routes and space for movement within a building. / egress: Movement out of a building or space, particularly for emergency exit. / accessibility: Usability of spaces and facilities by people with differing abilities. / sightline: A line of vision from a viewing position to an object or area. / acoustic performance: How a space handles sound for its intended use. / reverberation: Persistence of sound after the original source stops. / speech intelligibility: How clearly spoken words can be understood. / audiovisual (AV) system: Equipment for presenting or transmitting sound and images. / microphone coverage: The area from which a microphone system can capture usable sound. / power provision: The planned availability and location of electrical power. / storage allowance: Space reserved for items that are not currently in use. / adjacency: The relationship between nearby spaces or functions. / performance target: A desired measurable result that must still be evaluated. / design constraint: A condition limiting the available design choices.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation, distinguishing 12 workshop places from 24 presentation seats. Switch roles and repeat turns 5-12; emphasize the 15-minute target, storage, and hearing questions from the whole room. Read the checked transfer twice using 10, 18, and 20. Keep requested capacity distinct from approved occupancy.
Partner: Amira: Client representative / Ben: Architect
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: A separate client requests workshops for 10 people and presentations for 18, at different times. The desired changeover is 20 minutes. None of the capacities is approved.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Amira: What will you bring back to our next meeting? I need something concrete enough for staff to discuss without assuming the design is already settled.
Ben: Two proposed layouts and an explicit list of design constraints. I will identify the outstanding dimensional, storage, access, AV, code, and budget reviews beside the requested performance.
Amira: That works. Please preserve the twelve and twenty-four figures, different-time use, and fifteen-minute target, while clearly showing that feasibility remains to be established.
Ben: Agreed. Those will form the recorded functional requirements. The next step is to test them against the actual room and project constraints, not to label them approved capabilities.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

02Drawings, Specifications, and RFIsTwo documents disagree about the same finish+

Your goal

Describe a document discrepancy and request a traceable decision without inventing precedence.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

Two documents disagree about the same finish

Site coordinator Lena finds a finish discrepancy in Room 205. Drawing A601 revision C identifies wall finish F2; specification S09 revision B identifies F3 for the same wall. Both are in the current project issue. Neither document resolves the conflict, and no rule of precedence has been established in the supplied case. Architect Marco will coordinate a formal response. A project hold applies to the affected finish procurement until the discrepancy is resolved through the authorized process. The requested response date is Tuesday at 14:00, not a promised answer or automatic release.

Lena: Site coordinator / Marco: Architect

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Where is the discrepancy?

Answer and explanation

D. The same wall in Room 205

Both references identify different finishes for the same wall in Room 205.

2. Which finish can Lena select from the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

B. Neither; an authorized clarification is required.

The case supplies no precedence rule or authorized decision resolving the conflict.

3. What does Tuesday at 14:00 mean?

Answer and explanation

A. The requested response time

The requested date communicates a need without guaranteeing an answer or lifting the hold.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Revision letters apply within their own document histories. Revision C of one drawing is not automatically more authoritative than revision B of a different specification. Establish the applicable documents and the authorized resolution.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
request for information (RFI)
A documented request to clarify missing or conflicting project information.raise an RFI
drawing reference
The identifier used to locate a drawing or detail.cite the drawing reference
specification
Written requirements describing materials, quality, or execution.review the specification
revision
A controlled version of a document after changes.verify the revision
issue status
The purpose or authorization status assigned to a document release.check the issue status
finish schedule
A listing of finishes assigned to spaces or surfaces.consult the finish schedule
detail
An enlarged or specific representation of part of a design.reference the detail
elevation
A drawing showing a vertical face of a building or element.compare the elevation
section
A drawing showing a cut through a building or element.review the section
callout
A notation directing the reader to a detail or requirement.follow the callout
discrepancy
A difference between information that should agree.document the discrepancy
document precedence
The applicable order of authority when project documents conflict.establish document precedence
clarification
Information that resolves uncertainty about an existing requirement.obtain written clarification
design coordination
Aligning design information across documents and disciplines.coordinate the design response
submittal
Information submitted for the review required by the project process.track the submittal
shop drawing
A detailed drawing prepared for fabrication or installation.review the shop drawing
material sample
A physical example submitted to demonstrate a proposed material or finish.submit a material sample
substitution
A proposed alternative to the specified product or arrangement.request a substitution
procurement hold
A restriction on purchasing affected items pending a required decision.maintain the procurement hold
response date
The stated date requested or committed for a reply.distinguish the response date
RFI log
A register tracking information requests and their status.update the RFI log
superseded document
A document replaced by a later applicable issue.identify a superseded document
controlled distribution
A process for providing the correct document versions to recipients.maintain controlled distribution
traceability
The ability to follow a decision back to its source and record.preserve decision traceability

An RFI response is not automatically permission for added cost or time. If the answer changes the work, route its consequences through the applicable contract process. Do not silently convert clarification into a change authorization.

C Notice the language

Challenge the documents, not the person

A precise discrepancy report can be direct without accusing the designer of carelessness. Give the location and both references, then state the decision needed. Avoid turning a question into an instruction by writing a preferred answer as though it were already approved.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Locate the issue
The discrepancy concerns the same wall in Room 205.
Cite the drawing
A601 revision C identifies finish F2.
Cite the specification
S09 revision B identifies finish F3.
Confirm document status
Both references are in the current project issue.
Avoid invented precedence
The supplied information does not establish which requirement governs.
Ask a bounded question
Please confirm the required finish for this wall.
Attach supporting material
I will attach the marked location and both references.
Separate a proposal
Our suggested option is not an approved instruction.
State the current hold
Affected finish procurement remains on hold.
Explain the requested date
We request a response by Tuesday at 14:00.
Avoid an implied guarantee
That date is requested, not confirmed.
Track the reply
We will record the formal response in the RFI log.
Check change consequences
Any cost or time effect needs the applicable change process.
Distribute the decision
Please identify the documents that need updating.
Keep unaffected work distinct
This hold applies to the affected finish procurement.
Close with traceability
Link the decision to the RFI and the corrected document issue.
6 language and precision notes
Identifies
Reports what a document says without endorsing it as controlling.
For the same wall
Establishes that the references genuinely conflict.
Please confirm
Requests a specific decision, not general design advice.
Requested by
Does not mean promised by the receiving person.
Suggested
Marks an option as a proposal rather than authority.
Current versus controlling
A current document may still conflict with another current document.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which RFI description is strongest?

Answer and explanation

B. Room 205 wall: A601 rev C says F2; S09 rev B says F3. Please confirm the required finish.

The specific request identifies the location, conflict, references, and decision required.

2. What follows from both references being current?

Answer and explanation

A. The conflict still requires clarification.

Current issue status does not by itself resolve contradictory requirements.

3. What should happen if the reply adds cost or time?

Answer and explanation

A. Use the applicable change-authorization process.

Clarification and authorization of changed commercial effects are distinct project decisions.

4. Which update is supported before a reply arrives?

Answer and explanation

A. RFI open; affected finish procurement on hold; response requested Tuesday at 14:00.

This update preserves the actual open status, limited hold, and requested response time.

Two documents disagree about the same finish

Lena: Site coordinator / Marco: Architect

  8. Marco

  9. Lena

  10. Marco
    Record that as the requested 5. I have not committed to that time. If coordination takes longer, we must communicate the status rather than silently release the purchase.
  11. Lena
    I will make the subject specific: Room 205 wall finish discrepancy. The question will ask you to confirm the required finish, with both references attached.
  12. Marco
    That will make the 6 useful to the next person. Include the affected procurement item and current hold, without implying that every activity on site has stopped.
  13. Lena

  14. Marco

  15. Lena

  16. Marco

  17. Lena

  18. Marco
    Exactly. The goal is 9, not simply an email saying resolved. A later reviewer should be able to find the question, authorized response, and resulting document changes.
  19. Lena
    For now, the RFI stays open and the affected procurement stays on hold. Tuesday at fourteen hundred is our requested response time, not a release deadline.
  20. Marco
    Correct. Maintain the 10 until the authorized process resolves the issue. I will coordinate the response and make any remaining uncertainty or separate change requirement explicit.

Answers and explanations
  1. document references Turn 2. Document references identify the exact sources of the conflict so the response can address the same location and information.
  2. clarification Turn 4. Clarification resolves the uncertain requirement; the current documents alone do not establish whether F2 or F3 is correct.
  3. document precedence Turn 6. Document precedence concerns the applicable authority of conflicting documents, which has not been established in the supplied case.
  4. substitution Turn 8. A substitution is a proposed alternative to the requirement; preference or availability does not establish approval.
  5. response date Turn 10. The response date is requested here, not a promise by Marco or a condition that automatically releases procurement.
  6. RFI log Turn 12. The RFI log tracks the request, its references, response, and current status so another person can follow the decision.
  7. change authorization Turn 14. Change authorization addresses permission for altered work and its relevant effects; it is not automatically supplied by an RFI clarification.
  8. controlled distribution Turn 16. Controlled distribution provides recipients with the applicable document issue and helps prevent use of outdated conflicting information.
  9. traceability Turn 18. Traceability allows the team to follow the final requirement back through the documented decision and affected references.
  10. procurement hold Turn 20. The procurement hold remains in effect for the affected finish until the authorized process resolves the discrepancy and permits the next action.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Two finish descriptions for one corridor

Fictional project coordination: drawing A14 and specification section F2 describe different corridor finishes; neither precedence nor a final selection is established.

  1. Site coordinator: Drawing A14 shows one corridor finish, but specification section F2 describes a different one.
  2. Architectural coordinator: Please raise a request for information identifying the corridor and both document references.
  3. Site coordinator: Could you confirm the required finish before we tell the subcontractor what to order?
  4. Architectural coordinator: I'll route the RFI for an authorized response. I can't resolve the conflict from this summary alone.
  5. Site coordinator: Would using the drawing automatically settle it because the site team normally reads drawings first?
  6. Architectural coordinator: No. Reading habits don't establish document precedence or the project's approved finish selection.
  7. Site coordinator: I'll include the drawing set revision and relevant specification excerpt so the reviewer can compare them.
  8. Architectural coordinator: Good. Explain that procurement needs clarification, and ask when a reviewed response can be provided.
  9. Site coordinator: I'll keep the related submittal pending rather than describe either finish as approved.
  10. Architectural coordinator: That makes the request actionable and preserves the unresolved selection until the proper response arrives.
The submittal refers to a missing revision

Separate fictional case: a product submittal cites drawing revision C, while the coordination folder contains only revision B; procurement has not been authorized.

  1. Procurement coordinator: This submittal cites revision C, but our drawing set only includes revision B.
  2. Document controller: Could you send the submittal reference so I can check whether C was issued to this team?
  3. Procurement coordinator: I'll provide it. We need the applicable drawing before checking the product against the design.
  4. Document controller: Agreed. A revision letter in a supplier document doesn't establish that the drawing was formally issued.
  5. Procurement coordinator: Can I ask the supplier to change the letter to B and keep the review moving?
  6. Document controller: Not without clarification. That might conceal a genuine design change or an incorrect reference.
  7. Procurement coordinator: Then please verify the issued revision and identify who can clarify the submittal's basis.
  8. Document controller: I'll check the register and report what is available, including any missing transmission record.
  9. Procurement coordinator: I'll tell the review team why procurement remains pending instead of treating the mismatch as cosmetic.
  10. Document controller: Good. We'll preserve both references and resolve the document question through the project's review process.
Request a dimension without inventing one

Separate fictional coordination case: a joinery drawing omits a counter dimension needed for a shop-drawing review; no site measurement is supplied.

  1. Joinery coordinator: The counter depth is missing from drawing J6. Could you obtain the design dimension for our review?
  2. Design manager: Which counter and revision are you checking? The request needs to identify the exact missing information.
  3. Joinery coordinator: The reception counter on J6, revision A. Its depth isn't labeled in the drawing provided.
  4. Design manager: Please put those references in the RFI and explain that the shop-drawing review depends on clarification.
  5. Joinery coordinator: Could we scale it from the image and use that as the approved dimension?
  6. Design manager: We shouldn't turn an estimate from an image into an authorized design requirement.
  7. Joinery coordinator: Understood. I'll request the dimension and ask when the responsible designer can respond.
  8. Design manager: If the review timing is affected, state that impact without inventing a guaranteed response date.
  9. Joinery coordinator: I'll keep the submittal's dimension check open and note that no verified dimension is available.
  10. Design manager: That is a clear request: exact location, missing information, purpose, and a realistic timing question.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Lesson 02: Drawings, Specifications, and RFIs
Original fictional case: Site coordinator Lena finds a finish discrepancy in Room 205. Drawing A601 revision C identifies wall finish F2; specification S09 revision B identifies F3 for the same wall. Both are in the current project issue. Neither document resolves the conflict, and no rule of precedence has been established in the supplied case. Architect Marco will coordinate a formal response. A project hold applies to the affected finish procurement until the discrepancy is resolved through the authorized process. The requested response date is Tuesday at 14:00, not a promised answer or automatic release.
Language workshop: Two documents disagree about the same finish
Communication goal: Describe a document discrepancy and request a traceable decision without inventing precedence.
Language guidance: Revision letters apply within their own document histories. Revision C of one drawing is not automatically more authoritative than revision B of a different specification. Establish the applicable documents and the authorized resolution.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The discrepancy concerns the same wall in Room 205. / A601 revision C identifies finish F2. / S09 revision B identifies finish F3. / Both references are in the current project issue. / The supplied information does not establish which requirement governs. / Please confirm the required finish for this wall. / I will attach the marked location and both references. / Our suggested option is not an approved instruction. / Affected finish procurement remains on hold. / We request a response by Tuesday at 14:00. / That date is requested, not confirmed. / We will record the formal response in the RFI log. / Any cost or time effect needs the applicable change process. / Please identify the documents that need updating. / This hold applies to the affected finish procurement. / Link the decision to the RFI and the corrected document issue.
Vocabulary: request for information (RFI): A documented request to clarify missing or conflicting project information. / drawing reference: The identifier used to locate a drawing or detail. / specification: Written requirements describing materials, quality, or execution. / revision: A controlled version of a document after changes. / issue status: The purpose or authorization status assigned to a document release. / finish schedule: A listing of finishes assigned to spaces or surfaces. / detail: An enlarged or specific representation of part of a design. / elevation: A drawing showing a vertical face of a building or element. / section: A drawing showing a cut through a building or element. / callout: A notation directing the reader to a detail or requirement. / discrepancy: A difference between information that should agree. / document precedence: The applicable order of authority when project documents conflict. / clarification: Information that resolves uncertainty about an existing requirement. / design coordination: Aligning design information across documents and disciplines. / submittal: Information submitted for the review required by the project process. / shop drawing: A detailed drawing prepared for fabrication or installation. / material sample: A physical example submitted to demonstrate a proposed material or finish. / substitution: A proposed alternative to the specified product or arrangement. / procurement hold: A restriction on purchasing affected items pending a required decision. / response date: The stated date requested or committed for a reply. / RFI log: A register tracking information requests and their status. / superseded document: A document replaced by a later applicable issue. / controlled distribution: A process for providing the correct document versions to recipients. / traceability: The ability to follow a decision back to its source and record.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue, stating each document identifier, revision, and finish separately. Switch roles for turns 11-20; distinguish the RFI response from cost authorization and the requested date from a promise. Read the checked Room 310 transfer twice, keeping C1 and C4 attached to the correct source.
Partner: Lena: Site coordinator / Marco: Architect
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Room 310 has a ceiling conflict: drawing A702 revision D says C1; specification S12 revision B says C4. Neither requirement has been confirmed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Lena: Please tell us which sheets or extracts the response replaces. The buyer has a saved copy, and I do not want the old finish ordered after clarification.
Marco: Exactly. The goal is traceability, not simply an email saying resolved. A later reviewer should be able to find the question, authorized response, and resulting document changes.
Lena: For now, the RFI stays open and the affected procurement stays on hold. Tuesday at fourteen hundred is our requested response time, not a release deadline.
Marco: Correct. Maintain the procurement hold until the authorized process resolves the issue. I will coordinate the response and make any remaining uncertainty or separate change requirement explicit.

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
