03Change Orders and Cost ControlA price request became an instruction in the minutes+

Your goal

Distinguish pricing, approval, and authorization while explaining a proposed change in cost and time.

This lesson in the book, page 22 ↗

A Read the situation

A price request became an instruction in the minutes

A client asks the project team to price an additional partition. The original contract sum is $100,000; previously approved changes total a net addition of $5,000. Estimator Noor proposes a further $6,200 and an estimated two working days for the partition, subject to review. Neither has been accepted. No instruction to proceed has been issued under this fictional project's authorization process. Manager Felix notices that draft minutes say the client approved the work. The client actually requested pricing only. Other contracts may provide different change mechanisms; this case supplies no alternative instruction.

Noor: Estimator / Felix: Project manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is the current approved contract sum?

Answer and explanation

D. $105,000

The original $100,000 plus the approved $5,000 addition equals $105,000.

2. What has the client authorized in this case?

Answer and explanation

A. Preparation of pricing only

The client requested a price, while the proposed cost and time remain unaccepted.

3. What is the status of the two working days?

Answer and explanation

C. An estimated effect subject to review

The estimator supplies a provisional time estimate, not an agreed contract adjustment.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The approved sum is $105,000. Adding the unaccepted $6,200 would produce $111,200 only as a hypothetical revised sum. Show the pending proposal separately so the reader can distinguish agreed value from possible exposure.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
change order
A formal agreed change under the applicable contract process.execute a change order
scope change
An alteration to the work or deliverables required.assess a scope change
proposal request
A request to submit a price or other terms for proposed work.issue a proposal request
contract sum
The monetary amount established by the construction contract and agreed adjustments.state the current contract sum
approved adjustment
A change accepted through the applicable approval process.record an approved adjustment
net addition
The overall increase after relevant additions and deductions.calculate the net addition
deductive change
A change that reduces the relevant price or scope.price a deductive change
proposed amount
A figure submitted for consideration rather than accepted.separate the proposed amount
cost breakdown
An itemized explanation of the elements of a price.request a cost breakdown
quantity takeoff
Measurement of material or work quantities for estimating.check the quantity takeoff
labor allowance
An estimated amount assigned to labor in a price.review the labor allowance
material cost
The cost assigned to materials for the work.verify material costs
markup
An amount added to an underlying cost according to the pricing basis.explain the markup
contingency
An allowance for identified uncertainty or potential future cost.distinguish the contingency
exclusion
An item explicitly not included in a price or scope.identify a pricing exclusion
pricing assumption
A condition used as the basis of an estimate.state a pricing assumption
quotation validity
The period during which a quoted offer remains available on its stated terms.check quotation validity
time impact
The effect of a change or event on the project schedule.assess the time impact
working day
A day counted as working under the applicable project calendar.specify working days
authorization to proceed
Permission to begin specified work under the applicable process.verify authorization to proceed
change register
A record tracking proposed and approved changes.maintain the change register
pending exposure
A potential cost or obligation that has not been settled.report pending exposure
commitment
An obligation or undertaking, such as an approved purchase.distinguish a cost commitment
construction change directive
A contract mechanism directing a change under specified conditions before all terms are agreed.check a construction change directive

This fictional project has no instruction to proceed. Do not turn that fact into a universal rule that every contract requires an agreed change order before any changed work. Different contractual mechanisms require their own authority and documentation.

C Notice the language

Enthusiasm is not a commercial decision

A client saying that an idea looks useful may encourage a team to move quickly. Record the actual request and authority separately from the positive reaction. Accurate minutes protect shared understanding without requiring an accusatory conversation about who misunderstood whom.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Correct the minutes
The client requested pricing, not construction.
State the approved value
The current approved contract sum is one hundred five thousand dollars.
Separate the proposal
The additional six thousand two hundred dollars remains proposed.
Explain the conditional total
If accepted, that proposal would bring the sum to one hundred eleven thousand two hundred dollars.
Qualify the duration
The two working days are an estimate subject to review.
Keep time separate
No extension of contract time has been agreed.
Ask for the basis
Please show the quantities and pricing assumptions.
Check what is missing
Which items are excluded from this quotation?
Clarify markup
Please distinguish direct cost from the proposed markup.
Avoid implied authorization
Pricing the partition does not authorize construction.
Name the current boundary
No instruction to proceed has been issued in this case.
Track the status
Record the proposal as pending in the change register.
Preserve the original decision
The previously approved changes remain separate.
Address the deadline
Check quotation validity before relying on this price later.
Route the decision
Submit the proposal through the project's authorized process.
Close the correction
I will circulate corrected minutes identifying the request accurately.
6 language and precision notes
Would bring
Expresses a conditional total, not the present approved sum.
Net addition
Includes the effect of relevant deductions rather than adding every gross figure.
Subject to review
Preserves an unresolved assessment or decision.
Working days
Requires a calendar basis; it is not automatically elapsed calendar days.
Requested pricing
Specifies the action asked for without expanding it to execution.
No instruction issued
Describes the supplied case, not every possible contract mechanism.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which cost summary is correct?

Answer and explanation

C. Approved $105,000; proposed addition $6,200; hypothetical total $111,200

The summary preserves both the existing approved value and the separate unaccepted proposal.

2. Which minutes correction is appropriate?

Answer and explanation

B. Replace approved the partition with requested pricing for the partition.

The corrected wording matches the actual action requested rather than inventing an approval.

3. What does an estimated two-day effect establish?

Answer and explanation

A. A provisional schedule assessment

An estimate remains subject to review and does not itself amend contractual time.

4. Which statement respects different contracts?

Answer and explanation

A. Check the applicable mechanism and actual instruction; none is issued here.

The case describes one authorization status without imposing a universal rule on other contracts.

Check explanations in the book, page 89 ↗

E Conversations

A price request became an instruction in the minutes

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Noor: Estimator / Felix: Project manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • commitment
  • change register
  • net addition
  • pricing assumptions
  • proposal request
  • time impact
  • quotation validity
  • contract sum
  • cost breakdown
  • authorization to proceed
  1. Felix
    The draft minutes say the client approved the additional partition. I remember a request for pricing, not an instruction to build it. Does your record agree?
  2. Noor
    Yes. We received a 1. I prepared a price and provisional time estimate, but neither has been accepted through the project process.
  3. Felix
    Let's correct the minutes before the purchasing team relies on them. What is our current approved value, including the changes that were agreed earlier?
  4. Noor
    The original hundred thousand plus the approved five-thousand-dollar 2 gives us a hundred five thousand. The new partition proposal is separate.
  5. Felix
    And the six thousand two hundred for the partition would take that to a hundred eleven thousand two hundred only if it were accepted?
  6. Noor
    Correct. That is a conditional total, not the current 3. I will show the approved value, pending proposal, and hypothetical revised figure on separate lines.
  7. Felix
    Before I send the proposal, show me what the six thousand two hundred includes. Are labor, materials, and markup separated?
  8. Noor
    I will attach the 4, with quantities, labor and material allowances, markup, assumptions, and exclusions. The total alone does not explain the scope included in the offer.
  9. Felix
    The schedule note says two days. That could sound like we have already agreed an extension, especially if someone copies it without the qualification.
  10. Noor
    I will say estimated 5 of two working days, subject to review. We have not agreed any extension of contract time or confirmed how the work fits the sequence.
  11. Felix
    Please preserve working days as well. The client may read an unqualified two days as elapsed calendar time, which could produce a different expectation.
  12. Noor
    Agreed. I will also state the 6. If the scope or planned execution changes, we need to review whether the quotation still represents the work being requested.
  13. Felix
    Can procurement reserve materials now? They want to keep the option available, but a reservation might create an obligation even before installation begins.
  14. Noor
    We need to check the proposed 7 against the authorization process. In this case, no instruction to proceed has been issued, and I cannot authorize a purchase.
  15. Felix
    I will tell procurement that we have a pricing request, not an instruction to proceed. Flag any reservation charge before anyone commits us to it.
  16. Noor
    Exactly. The 8 should show this item as pending. It should not be merged into the approved total simply because the client sounded enthusiastic.
  17. Felix
    We should also check how long the price remains available. The client may defer the decision and return later expecting every figure to remain unchanged.
  18. Noor
    I will confirm the 9 and communicate it with the proposal. We should not invent a validity period or silently treat an expired offer as current.
  19. Felix
    I will circulate corrected minutes: pricing requested, six thousand two hundred proposed, two working days estimated, and no accepted cost or time adjustment.
  20. Noor
    Add that 10 remains absent under this project's process. That gives the team a clear current status without turning the proposal into a decision.

Answers and explanations
  1. proposal request Turn 2. A proposal request asks for terms or pricing; it is not the client's acceptance of the proposed work.
  2. net addition Turn 4. The net addition is the already approved increase, giving a current contract sum of $105,000 before the new proposal.
  3. contract sum Turn 6. The contract sum reflects the original agreement and approved adjustments, not a proposal still awaiting acceptance.
  4. cost breakdown Turn 8. The cost breakdown itemizes the elements behind the proposed price so the reviewer can examine its basis.
  5. time impact Turn 10. Time impact is the assessed schedule effect; the two-day figure remains an estimate rather than an agreed extension.
  6. pricing assumptions Turn 12. Pricing assumptions are the conditions underlying the estimate; changing them may alter what the quoted price covers.
  7. commitment Turn 14. A commitment can create an obligation such as a purchase; it must not be assumed permissible merely because the work is still proposed.
  8. change register Turn 16. The change register tracks the proposal separately from approved changes, preserving its pending status for later review.
  9. quotation validity Turn 18. Quotation validity identifies the period and terms under which the offer remains available, rather than assuming an estimate lasts indefinitely.
  10. authorization to proceed Turn 20. Authorization to proceed is permission to start specified work; the supplied case explicitly has no such instruction.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Felix: The draft minutes say the client approved the additional partition. I remember a request for pricing, not an instruction to build it. Does your record agree?
  2. Noor: Yes. We received a proposal request. I prepared a price and provisional time estimate, but neither has been accepted through the project process.
  3. Felix: Let's correct the minutes before the purchasing team relies on them. What is our current approved value, including the changes that were agreed earlier?
  4. Noor: The original hundred thousand plus the approved five-thousand-dollar net addition gives us a hundred five thousand. The new partition proposal is separate.
  5. Felix: And the six thousand two hundred for the partition would take that to a hundred eleven thousand two hundred only if it were accepted?
  6. Noor: Correct. That is a conditional total, not the current contract sum. I will show the approved value, pending proposal, and hypothetical revised figure on separate lines.
  7. Felix: Before I send the proposal, show me what the six thousand two hundred includes. Are labor, materials, and markup separated?
  8. Noor: I will attach the cost breakdown, with quantities, labor and material allowances, markup, assumptions, and exclusions. The total alone does not explain the scope included in the offer.
  9. Felix: The schedule note says two days. That could sound like we have already agreed an extension, especially if someone copies it without the qualification.
  10. Noor: I will say estimated time impact of two working days, subject to review. We have not agreed any extension of contract time or confirmed how the work fits the sequence.
  11. Felix: Please preserve working days as well. The client may read an unqualified two days as elapsed calendar time, which could produce a different expectation.
  12. Noor: Agreed. I will also state the pricing assumptions. If the scope or planned execution changes, we need to review whether the quotation still represents the work being requested.
  13. Felix: Can procurement reserve materials now? They want to keep the option available, but a reservation might create an obligation even before installation begins.
  14. Noor: We need to check the proposed commitment against the authorization process. In this case, no instruction to proceed has been issued, and I cannot authorize a purchase.
  15. Felix: I will tell procurement that we have a pricing request, not an instruction to proceed. Flag any reservation charge before anyone commits us to it.
  16. Noor: Exactly. The change register should show this item as pending. It should not be merged into the approved total simply because the client sounded enthusiastic.
  17. Felix: We should also check how long the price remains available. The client may defer the decision and return later expecting every figure to remain unchanged.
  18. Noor: I will confirm the quotation validity and communicate it with the proposal. We should not invent a validity period or silently treat an expired offer as current.
  19. Felix: I will circulate corrected minutes: pricing requested, six thousand two hundred proposed, two working days estimated, and no accepted cost or time adjustment.
  20. Noor: Add that authorization to proceed remains absent under this project's process. That gives the team a clear current status without turning the proposal into a decision.
Dialogue in the book, page 28 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Price the partition before promising it

Fictional change discussion: a client requests an extra partition after pricing is agreed; cost and schedule effects are unassessed.

  1. Client representative: Could you add another partition and still finish for the agreed price and date?
  2. Project manager: We can assess the addition, but neither its cost nor schedule effect has been reviewed.
  3. Client representative: There is a contingency in the budget. Doesn't that mean this change is already covered?
  4. Project manager: A contingency doesn't establish approval for this particular change or eliminate its possible timing effect.
  5. Client representative: What options can I take to the client team without promising something you can't deliver?
  6. Project manager: We can keep the agreed scope or seek a priced change order for the partition.
  7. Client representative: If we choose the review, does that authorize you to start the extra work?
  8. Project manager: No. Assessment and authorization remain separate under this project's change process.
  9. Client representative: I'll request the assessment and ask the client to decide after seeing cost and schedule implications.
  10. Project manager: I'll document the request and applicable notice steps without guaranteeing an unchanged budget or completion date.
An allowance is not a fixed supplier quote

Separate fictional case: a lobby project has a lighting allowance, and a client proposes a fixture that has not been quoted or reviewed.

  1. Client project lead: Can we select this feature light today? The budget already includes a lighting allowance.
  2. Cost consultant: The allowance is a budget provision, not a verified quote for this particular fixture.
  3. Client project lead: Then what can we decide now without delaying the entire design discussion this afternoon?
  4. Cost consultant: You can nominate it for review, while retaining the current selection as the comparison option.
  5. Client project lead: Would the review include supply timing as well as the purchase price from the vendor?
  6. Cost consultant: Yes, we should compare relevant cost and schedule effects before recommending a change.
  7. Client project lead: If it fits the allowance, can I tell the team the change order is automatically approved?
  8. Cost consultant: No. Budget fit doesn't replace the project's approval process or confirm all associated costs.
  9. Client project lead: I'll present it as a preferred candidate, conditional on the assessment and authorized approval.
  10. Cost consultant: I'll request the necessary information and keep the existing approved scope distinct from this proposal.
A phased change needs defined boundaries

Separate fictional case: a client requests repainting three additional rooms; the contractor can assess all three together or assess one priority room first. No extra work is approved.

  1. Facilities client: We want three more rooms repainted, but one is needed sooner than the others.
  2. Contract administrator: We can assess all three together or review the priority room first as a separate proposal.
  3. Facilities client: Would reviewing one first guarantee that it finishes before the original project handover date?
  4. Contract administrator: No. We still need the scope, access, cost, and schedule assessment for that room.
  5. Facilities client: Then I'd prefer the priority-room assessment first, provided the other two aren't silently removed.
  6. Contract administrator: We can record all three requests and identify the first room as the assessment priority.
  7. Facilities client: Does that mean the remaining rooms are included in the original contract allowance?
  8. Contract administrator: No inclusion is established. Each requested addition remains subject to the agreed change-order process.
  9. Facilities client: I'll explain that sequencing the assessment is different from approving the work or its funding.
  10. Contract administrator: Good. I'll record the bounded request and ask when the reviewing team can provide an assessment.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

A price request became an instruction in the minutes
  1. Read the corrected conversation with $105,000 approved and $6,200 proposed.
  2. Switch roles; stress would in the $111,200 total and estimated in the two-working-day effect.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Keep pending money separate

Another contract started at $80,000 and has approved net additions of $4,000. A new $3,000 proposal remains unaccepted. No time adjustment has been agreed.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Manager: "The current approved sum is $___."

Answer and explanation

A. 84,000

The original eighty thousand plus four thousand approved equals eighty-four thousand.

2. Estimator: "The pending proposal is $___."

Answer and explanation

D. 3,000

Three thousand is the separate proposal that remains unaccepted.

3. Manager: "If accepted, the revised sum would be $___."

Answer and explanation

A. 87,000

Adding the pending three thousand to eighty-four thousand gives eighty-seven thousand.

4. Estimator: "The proposed change remains ___."

Answer and explanation

C. unaccepted

The supplied facts explicitly say the new proposal has not been accepted.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.

Lesson 03: Change Orders and Cost Control
Original fictional case: A client asks the project team to price an additional partition. The original contract sum is $100,000; previously approved changes total a net addition of $5,000. Estimator Noor proposes a further $6,200 and an estimated two working days for the partition, subject to review. Neither has been accepted. No instruction to proceed has been issued under this fictional project's authorization process. Manager Felix notices that draft minutes say the client approved the work. The client actually requested pricing only. Other contracts may provide different change mechanisms; this case supplies no alternative instruction.
Language workshop: A price request became an instruction in the minutes
Communication goal: Distinguish pricing, approval, and authorization while explaining a proposed change in cost and time.
Language guidance: The approved sum is $105,000. Adding the unaccepted $6,200 would produce $111,200 only as a hypothetical revised sum. Show the pending proposal separately so the reader can distinguish agreed value from possible exposure.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The client requested pricing, not construction. / The current approved contract sum is one hundred five thousand dollars. / The additional six thousand two hundred dollars remains proposed. / If accepted, that proposal would bring the sum to one hundred eleven thousand two hundred dollars. / The two working days are an estimate subject to review. / No extension of contract time has been agreed. / Please show the quantities and pricing assumptions. / Which items are excluded from this quotation? / Please distinguish direct cost from the proposed markup. / Pricing the partition does not authorize construction. / No instruction to proceed has been issued in this case. / Record the proposal as pending in the change register. / The previously approved changes remain separate. / Check quotation validity before relying on this price later. / Submit the proposal through the project's authorized process. / I will circulate corrected minutes identifying the request accurately.
Vocabulary: change order: A formal agreed change under the applicable contract process. / scope change: An alteration to the work or deliverables required. / proposal request: A request to submit a price or other terms for proposed work. / contract sum: The monetary amount established by the construction contract and agreed adjustments. / approved adjustment: A change accepted through the applicable approval process. / net addition: The overall increase after relevant additions and deductions. / deductive change: A change that reduces the relevant price or scope. / proposed amount: A figure submitted for consideration rather than accepted. / cost breakdown: An itemized explanation of the elements of a price. / quantity takeoff: Measurement of material or work quantities for estimating. / labor allowance: An estimated amount assigned to labor in a price. / material cost: The cost assigned to materials for the work. / markup: An amount added to an underlying cost according to the pricing basis. / contingency: An allowance for identified uncertainty or potential future cost. / exclusion: An item explicitly not included in a price or scope. / pricing assumption: A condition used as the basis of an estimate. / quotation validity: The period during which a quoted offer remains available on its stated terms. / time impact: The effect of a change or event on the project schedule. / working day: A day counted as working under the applicable project calendar. / authorization to proceed: Permission to begin specified work under the applicable process. / change register: A record tracking proposed and approved changes. / pending exposure: A potential cost or obligation that has not been settled. / commitment: An obligation or undertaking, such as an approved purchase. / construction change directive: A contract mechanism directing a change under specified conditions before all terms are agreed.
Speaking task: Read the corrected conversation with $105,000 approved and $6,200 proposed. Switch roles; stress would in the $111,200 total and estimated in the two-working-day effect. Read the checked transfer twice. Keep $84,000 current and $87,000 conditional on acceptance.
Partner: Noor: Estimator / Felix: Project manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Another contract started at $80,000 and has approved net additions of $4,000. A new $3,000 proposal remains unaccepted. No time adjustment has been agreed.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Felix: We should also check how long the price remains available. The client may defer the decision and return later expecting every figure to remain unchanged.
Noor: I will confirm the quotation validity and communicate it with the proposal. We should not invent a validity period or silently treat an expired offer as current.
Felix: I will circulate corrected minutes: pricing requested, six thousand two hundred proposed, two working days estimated, and no accepted cost or time adjustment.
Noor: Add that authorization to proceed remains absent under this project's process. That gives the team a clear current status without turning the proposal into a decision.
END REFERENCE

Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

04Schedule, Sequencing, and Critical PathThe lookahead still shows a delivery that cannot happen+

Your goal

Explain a constrained sequence and distinguish forecast movement from contractual entitlement.

This lesson in the book, page 31 ↗

A Read the situation

The lookahead still shows a delivery that cannot happen

A simplified project calendar uses numbered working days. The baseline shows delivery at the end of Day 1, installation on Days 2 and 3, and testing on Day 4. Each task depends on the previous one finishing; no overlap or float is available in this supplied sequence. Delivery is now forecast at the end of Day 3. Installation still needs two full working days, followed by one testing day. Planner Imani and site manager Owen have not verified any recovery option. The lookahead still shows the original dates. This arithmetic is not a contract-extension decision.

Imani: Planner / Owen: Site manager

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. With the stated dependencies, when can installation occur?

Answer and explanation

A. Days 4 and 5

Delivery finishes at the end of Day 3, so two full installation days follow.

2. When does testing move to?

Answer and explanation

A. Day 6

Testing requires completed installation, which now finishes at the end of Day 5.

3. What has been established about recovery?

Answer and explanation

D. No recovery option has been verified.

The brief provides no verified way to shorten or overlap the activities.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The delivery moves from the end of Day 1 to the end of Day 3. With unchanged durations and no overlap, installation moves to Days 4 and 5, and testing to Day 6. The forecast shifts two working days.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
baseline schedule
The agreed reference plan used for comparison with later progress.retain the baseline schedule
lookahead schedule
A short-term plan of upcoming activities and requirements.update the lookahead schedule
critical path
A sequence of activities determining the relevant earliest completion under the schedule logic.review the critical path
predecessor
An activity that must occur before a linked activity under the schedule logic.identify the predecessor
successor
An activity linked to follow another under the schedule logic.identify the successor
finish-to-start relationship
A link requiring one activity to finish before the next starts.model a finish-to-start relationship
activity duration
The time required to perform a scheduled activity.verify the activity duration
total float
Time an activity can move without delaying the relevant project completion under the schedule model.assess total float
free float
Time an activity can move without delaying the early start of a successor.distinguish free float
lead time
The time needed to obtain an item or complete a preparatory process.confirm the lead time
forecast date
The date currently expected on the basis of available information.revise the forecast date
data date
The point in time to which schedule progress information is updated.state the data date
milestone
A significant scheduled event, usually with no duration.track the testing milestone
constraint
A restriction affecting when or how an activity may occur.identify a schedule constraint
workfront
An area or package available for a crew to perform work.release a workfront
sequence
The order in which activities are carried out.preserve the required sequence
recovery plan
A proposal for regaining lost time or limiting delay.evaluate a recovery plan
acceleration
Measures intended to complete work sooner than otherwise forecast.assess acceleration options
resource availability
The availability of labor, equipment, or other required inputs.verify resource availability
productivity
The output achieved per unit of input or time.test productivity assumptions
schedule variance
A difference between planned and actual or forecast timing.explain the schedule variance
critical-path analysis
Evaluation of schedule logic and the activities driving completion.update the critical-path analysis
extension of time
A contractual adjustment to the allowed completion period when properly established.distinguish an extension of time
calendar basis
The working and nonworking periods used to count schedule time.state the calendar basis

A schedule forecast describes expected timing under stated assumptions. It does not by itself establish responsibility for delay, entitlement to extra time, or entitlement to payment. Recovery proposals also require evidence, resources, and appropriate approval.

C Notice the language

A changed forecast is information, not disloyalty

A team may feel that moving a date admits defeat. Keeping an obsolete plan visible as the current forecast is more misleading. Preserve the baseline for comparison, show the supported forecast, and evaluate recovery options separately from promises.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Identify the reference
The baseline places testing on Day 4.
State the changed input
Delivery is now forecast for the end of Day 3.
Explain the dependency
Installation cannot start until delivery is complete.
Preserve the duration
Installation still requires two full working days.
Calculate the new sequence
Installation moves to Days 4 and 5.
State the resulting forecast
Testing moves to Day 6 under the supplied logic.
Quantify the variance
That is a two-working-day movement from the baseline.
Keep the reference intact
Retain the baseline and show the revised forecast separately.
Correct the short-term plan
The lookahead must reflect the current delivery forecast.
Avoid unsupported recovery
We have not verified an option that restores Day 4.
Check resources
Extra hours are not a recovery plan until their feasibility is assessed.
Protect the dependency
We cannot assume testing overlaps unfinished installation.
Qualify the model
This result follows the simplified sequence supplied here.
Separate contractual questions
The forecast does not itself grant an extension of time.
State the clock basis
These are numbered working days, not calendar dates.
Close with the next update
I will issue the revised lookahead with its assumptions.
6 language and precision notes
At the end of
Matters because the successor cannot use the same full working day.
Still requires
Keeps the activity duration unchanged despite pressure.
Moves to
Reports a forecast change without assigning legal responsibility.
No float
Removes unused schedule allowance in the supplied model.
Under the supplied logic
Limits the calculation to its stated assumptions.
Restore versus request
A proposed recovery action does not prove the old date can be restored.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which sequence follows the supplied facts?

Answer and explanation

D. Delivery ends Day 3; installation Days 4-5; testing Day 6

The finish-to-start links and unchanged durations produce testing on Day 6.

2. How should the schedule records be handled?

Answer and explanation

A. Preserve the baseline and update the current lookahead.

The baseline supports comparison while the lookahead must represent the supported upcoming sequence.

3. What is the variance in testing?

Answer and explanation

B. Two working days later

Testing moves from Day 4 to Day 6, a difference of two working days.

4. Which contractual conclusion is supported?

Answer and explanation

B. None is established by this calculation alone.

Forecast arithmetic alone does not determine contractual responsibility, relief, or payment.

E Conversations

The lookahead still shows a delivery that cannot happen

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Imani: Planner / Owen: Site manager

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • extension of time
  • lookahead schedule
  • forecast date
  • baseline schedule
  • resource availability
  • finish-to-start relationship
  • calendar basis
  • recovery plan
  • activity duration
  • schedule variance
  1. Owen
    The delivery update says the end of Day 3, but our lookahead still shows installation on Days 2 and 3. The crews are asking which plan applies.
  2. Imani
    The 1 needs correcting. We should keep the baseline for comparison, but it cannot remain our current forecast when a necessary input has moved.
  3. Owen
    Walk me through the sequence before I brief them. Delivery originally finished at the end of Day 1, installation took two days, and testing followed.
  4. Imani
    Correct. The 2 means installation cannot begin before delivery finishes. With delivery now ending on Day 3, installation uses Days 4 and 5.
  5. Owen
    So testing moves to Day 6, provided the installation duration stays at two full working days and there is no other change to the supplied sequence.
  6. Imani
    Exactly. The 3 has not shortened. We have no overlap or float available in this model, so the delivery movement passes through to the testing date.
  7. Owen
    The installer has been asked to catch up and keep testing on Day 4. Has anyone checked a workable way to do that?
  8. Imani
    It is not a verified 4. We would need to assess the proposed method, resources, productivity, and constraints before making a commitment based on a shorter duration.
  9. Owen
    Extra hours are being discussed. I do not know whether the crew, equipment, access arrangements, or necessary supervision would actually be available for them.
  10. Imani
    Then 5 remains an open question. We should label acceleration as an option under review, not assume it restores the original testing date without consequences.
  11. Owen
    I also want to avoid confusion about days. This example uses numbered working days, not dates on a calendar with a weekend somewhere in the middle.
  12. Imani
    State the 6 explicitly. Day 6 is two working days later than Day 4. We should not translate that into calendar dates without the project calendar.
  13. Owen
    Please keep the original Day 4 alongside the forecast. The client will want to see the movement, not just a replacement date.
  14. Imani
    Retain the 7 and show the forecast beside it. That makes the two-day movement visible instead of hiding it by overwriting the original reference.
  15. Owen
    Then the client sees both the original testing point and the expected one. We can explain the dependency rather than present a date with no basis.
  16. Imani
    Yes. Describe the 8 and its assumptions. If the delivery forecast changes again, or a recovery option becomes verified, update the analysis rather than reuse this result automatically.
  17. Owen
    Does this mean we can state that a two-day extension of time has been approved? The project report has a column asking about contractual completion.
  18. Imani
    No. An 9 is a separate contractual question. This calculation establishes neither entitlement nor responsibility, and it does not approve payment or an adjusted contract completion date.
  19. Owen
    I will brief installation on Days 4 and 5 and testing on Day 6 under the supplied assumptions, with recovery still unverified and contractual questions separate.
  20. Imani
    I will issue that 10 in the revised lookahead and retain the baseline comparison. The record will show the changed delivery input and the unchanged activity durations clearly.

Answers and explanations
  1. lookahead schedule Turn 2. The lookahead schedule is the short-term upcoming plan and must reflect the changed delivery forecast rather than obsolete dates.
  2. finish-to-start relationship Turn 4. The finish-to-start relationship requires the preceding activity to finish before its successor begins, so installation follows the completed delivery.
  3. activity duration Turn 6. Activity duration is the time needed for the task; installation remains two full working days in the supplied facts.
  4. recovery plan Turn 8. A recovery plan needs a feasible method for regaining time; simply retaining the old target does not demonstrate recovery.
  5. resource availability Turn 10. Resource availability concerns whether the people, equipment, and other inputs needed for a recovery proposal can actually be provided.
  6. calendar basis Turn 12. The calendar basis identifies which periods count for scheduling; here all numbered days are working days rather than unqualified calendar days.
  7. baseline schedule Turn 14. The baseline schedule remains the reference plan for comparing the original timing with the current forecast.
  8. schedule variance Turn 16. Schedule variance is the difference between the reference and updated timing, here testing moving from Day 4 to Day 6.
  9. extension of time Turn 18. An extension of time is a contractual adjustment requiring the applicable basis and process, not an automatic consequence of forecast arithmetic.
  10. forecast date Turn 20. The forecast date states the currently expected timing under the available information, while retaining uncertainty and the separate baseline reference.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Owen: The delivery update says the end of Day 3, but our lookahead still shows installation on Days 2 and 3. The crews are asking which plan applies.
  2. Imani: The lookahead schedule needs correcting. We should keep the baseline for comparison, but it cannot remain our current forecast when a necessary input has moved.
  3. Owen: Walk me through the sequence before I brief them. Delivery originally finished at the end of Day 1, installation took two days, and testing followed.
  4. Imani: Correct. The finish-to-start relationship means installation cannot begin before delivery finishes. With delivery now ending on Day 3, installation uses Days 4 and 5.
  5. Owen: So testing moves to Day 6, provided the installation duration stays at two full working days and there is no other change to the supplied sequence.
  6. Imani: Exactly. The activity duration has not shortened. We have no overlap or float available in this model, so the delivery movement passes through to the testing date.
  7. Owen: The installer has been asked to catch up and keep testing on Day 4. Has anyone checked a workable way to do that?
  8. Imani: It is not a verified recovery plan. We would need to assess the proposed method, resources, productivity, and constraints before making a commitment based on a shorter duration.
  9. Owen: Extra hours are being discussed. I do not know whether the crew, equipment, access arrangements, or necessary supervision would actually be available for them.
  10. Imani: Then resource availability remains an open question. We should label acceleration as an option under review, not assume it restores the original testing date without consequences.
  11. Owen: I also want to avoid confusion about days. This example uses numbered working days, not dates on a calendar with a weekend somewhere in the middle.
  12. Imani: State the calendar basis explicitly. Day 6 is two working days later than Day 4. We should not translate that into calendar dates without the project calendar.
  13. Owen: Please keep the original Day 4 alongside the forecast. The client will want to see the movement, not just a replacement date.
  14. Imani: Retain the baseline schedule and show the forecast beside it. That makes the two-day movement visible instead of hiding it by overwriting the original reference.
  15. Owen: Then the client sees both the original testing point and the expected one. We can explain the dependency rather than present a date with no basis.
  16. Imani: Yes. Describe the schedule variance and its assumptions. If the delivery forecast changes again, or a recovery option becomes verified, update the analysis rather than reuse this result automatically.
  17. Owen: Does this mean we can state that a two-day extension of time has been approved? The project report has a column asking about contractual completion.
  18. Imani: No. An extension of time is a separate contractual question. This calculation establishes neither entitlement nor responsibility, and it does not approve payment or an adjusted contract completion date.
  19. Owen: I will brief installation on Days 4 and 5 and testing on Day 6 under the supplied assumptions, with recovery still unverified and contractual questions separate.
  20. Imani: I will issue that forecast date in the revised lookahead and retain the baseline comparison. The record will show the changed delivery input and the unchanged activity durations clearly.
Dialogue in the book, page 37 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The lookahead still shows the old delivery

Fictional scheduling exercise: a delayed delivery affects a task on the current critical path; the lookahead has not been updated and recovery options are unassessed.

  1. Planner: The delivery delay affects a critical-path task, but our lookahead still shows the original sequence.
  2. Site manager: Have you completed the revised schedule, or are you only identifying the impact at this stage?
  3. Planner: I've identified the affected task. The revised sequence and any recovery options remain under review.
  4. Site manager: Can I tell the client that resequencing will protect the original completion date?
  5. Planner: Not yet. We haven't established a workable recovery plan or its effect on the critical path.
  6. Site manager: What can we say now that is more useful than simply reporting a supplier delay?
  7. Planner: The delivery is delayed, the current sequence is affected, and the lookahead revision is pending.
  8. Site manager: Please identify the review owner and keep any available float separate from an assumed recovery.
  9. Planner: I'll own the scheduling review and ask the delivery team for a verified arrival update.
  10. Site manager: Good. The next briefing should distinguish completed impact identification from the unresolved recovery assessment.
Available float is not approved resequencing

Separate fictional project case: a planner identifies two days of float on an internal finish task; a proposed resequence still needs coordination review.

  1. Construction manager: This finish task has two days of float. Can we announce the revised sequence as approved?
  2. Scheduler: No. I've checked the current schedule allowance, but the proposed resequencing still needs coordination review.
  3. Construction manager: What remains open if the schedule already shows that amount of flexibility for the task?
  4. Scheduler: The affected teams haven't confirmed access and dependencies. Float alone doesn't resolve those practical questions.
  5. Construction manager: Then the completed item is your schedule check, not agreement by everyone involved in the change.
  6. Scheduler: Exactly. The lookahead can show the proposal clearly, but shouldn't present it as the agreed plan.
  7. Construction manager: Could we omit the pending status to avoid confusing the subcontractors during tomorrow's briefing?
  8. Scheduler: That would create greater confusion if they act on a sequence nobody has accepted.
  9. Construction manager: I'll ask the coordinators for their responses and describe the two-day float as a current schedule finding.
  10. Scheduler: I'll update the lookahead after those reviews and preserve any unresolved dependencies in the status summary.
Delivered material, unfinished readiness check

Separate fictional case: ceiling panels have arrived, but the area-readiness review for their scheduled task remains open.

  1. Logistics coordinator: The ceiling panels arrived this morning. Is the related lookahead task now ready to proceed?
  2. Site planner: Delivery is complete, but the area-readiness review is still open with the responsible team.
  3. Logistics coordinator: The progress slide currently says all constraints removed. That seems broader than the delivery update.
  4. Site planner: It is. We should correct it to separate material availability from the unresolved readiness check.
  5. Logistics coordinator: Would that mean the delivery wasn't useful progress after all for the critical-path discussion?
  6. Site planner: It is useful progress. It just doesn't establish that every dependency for the task is resolved.
  7. Logistics coordinator: I'll confirm the received quantity in our record and leave the readiness decision to its owner.
  8. Site planner: Thank you. I'll ask that team for its status before revising the sequence in the lookahead.
  9. Logistics coordinator: So the update is panels delivered, readiness pending, and schedule implications still being checked?
  10. Site planner: Correct. That reports what changed without treating a completed delivery as permission to begin work.

Follow the dependency chain

Delivery finishes at the end of Day 5. Installation needs two full working days after delivery. Testing takes the following full day. No overlap is available.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Planner: "Installation begins on Day ___."

Answer and explanation

C. 6

The first full working day after the end of Day 5 is Day 6.

2. Manager: "Its second day is Day ___."

Answer and explanation

B. 7

Two full installation days are Days 6 and 7.

3. Planner: "Testing therefore occurs on Day ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 8

Testing follows completed installation, so it occurs on Day 8.

4. Manager: "The dependency permits no ___."

Answer and explanation

A. overlap

The supplied facts expressly rule out overlapping the activities.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Original fictional case: A simplified project calendar uses numbered working days. The baseline shows delivery at the end of Day 1, installation on Days 2 and 3, and testing on Day 4. Optional second-round challenge: Delivery finishes at the end of Day 5. Installation needs two full working days after delivery. Testing takes the following full day. No overlap is available.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: Does this mean we can state that a two-day extension of time has been approved? The project report has a column asking about contractual completion.
Imani: No. An extension of time is a separate contractual question. This calculation establishes neither entitlement nor responsibility, and it does not approve payment or an adjusted contract completion date.
Owen: I will brief installation on Days 4 and 5 and testing on Day 6 under the supplied assumptions, with recovery still unverified and contractual questions separate.
Imani: I will issue that forecast date in the revised lookahead and retain the baseline comparison. The record will show the changed delivery input and the unchanged activity durations clearly.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 04: Schedule, Sequencing, and Critical Path
Original fictional case: A simplified project calendar uses numbered working days. The baseline shows delivery at the end of Day 1, installation on Days 2 and 3, and testing on Day 4. Each task depends on the previous one finishing; no overlap or float is available in this supplied sequence. Delivery is now forecast at the end of Day 3. Installation still needs two full working days, followed by one testing day. Planner Imani and site manager Owen have not verified any recovery option. The lookahead still shows the original dates. This arithmetic is not a contract-extension decision.
Language workshop: The lookahead still shows a delivery that cannot happen
Communication goal: Explain a constrained sequence and distinguish forecast movement from contractual entitlement.
Language guidance: The delivery moves from the end of Day 1 to the end of Day 3. With unchanged durations and no overlap, installation moves to Days 4 and 5, and testing to Day 6. The forecast shifts two working days.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): The baseline places testing on Day 4. / Delivery is now forecast for the end of Day 3. / Installation cannot start until delivery is complete. / Installation still requires two full working days. / Installation moves to Days 4 and 5. / Testing moves to Day 6 under the supplied logic. / That is a two-working-day movement from the baseline. / Retain the baseline and show the revised forecast separately. / The lookahead must reflect the current delivery forecast. / We have not verified an option that restores Day 4. / Extra hours are not a recovery plan until their feasibility is assessed. / We cannot assume testing overlaps unfinished installation. / This result follows the simplified sequence supplied here. / The forecast does not itself grant an extension of time. / These are numbered working days, not calendar dates. / I will issue the revised lookahead with its assumptions.
Vocabulary: baseline schedule: The agreed reference plan used for comparison with later progress. / lookahead schedule: A short-term plan of upcoming activities and requirements. / critical path: A sequence of activities determining the relevant earliest completion under the schedule logic. / predecessor: An activity that must occur before a linked activity under the schedule logic. / successor: An activity linked to follow another under the schedule logic. / finish-to-start relationship: A link requiring one activity to finish before the next starts. / activity duration: The time required to perform a scheduled activity. / total float: Time an activity can move without delaying the relevant project completion under the schedule model. / free float: Time an activity can move without delaying the early start of a successor. / lead time: The time needed to obtain an item or complete a preparatory process. / forecast date: The date currently expected on the basis of available information. / data date: The point in time to which schedule progress information is updated. / milestone: A significant scheduled event, usually with no duration. / constraint: A restriction affecting when or how an activity may occur. / workfront: An area or package available for a crew to perform work. / sequence: The order in which activities are carried out. / recovery plan: A proposal for regaining lost time or limiting delay. / acceleration: Measures intended to complete work sooner than otherwise forecast. / resource availability: The availability of labor, equipment, or other required inputs. / productivity: The output achieved per unit of input or time. / schedule variance: A difference between planned and actual or forecast timing. / critical-path analysis: Evaluation of schedule logic and the activities driving completion. / extension of time: A contractual adjustment to the allowed completion period when properly established. / calendar basis: The working and nonworking periods used to count schedule time.
Speaking task: Read the corrected dialogue with delivery ending Day 3, installation Days 4-5, and testing Day 6. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Retain working days and keep a forecast separate from a contractual extension. Read the checked transfer twice using Days 6, 7, and 8 without overlapping the activities.
Partner: Imani: Planner / Owen: Site manager
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Delivery finishes at the end of Day 5. Installation needs two full working days after delivery. Testing takes the following full day. No overlap is available.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Owen: Does this mean we can state that a two-day extension of time has been approved? The project report has a column asking about contractual completion.
Imani: No. An extension of time is a separate contractual question. This calculation establishes neither entitlement nor responsibility, and it does not approve payment or an adjusted contract completion date.
Owen: I will brief installation on Days 4 and 5 and testing on Day 6 under the supplied assumptions, with recovery still unverified and contractual questions separate.
Imani: I will issue that forecast date in the revised lookahead and retain the baseline comparison. The record will show the changed delivery input and the unchanged activity durations clearly.

05Site Safety and Toolbox TalksThe briefing says use the right protection+

Your goal

Request task-specific, approved safety information and confirm understanding without inventing a work method.

This lesson in the book, page 40 ↗

A Read the situation

The briefing says use the right protection

A crew is due to cut composite panels with a powered tool in Zone B. The briefing card says only use the right protection. It does not identify the approved task method, controls, or equipment requirements. Foreperson Rosa has stopped the affected task under the fictional site process. Safety coordinator Dev will obtain qualified review and the applicable approved briefing before any authorized restart. No one is being asked to select equipment from this book. Similar work performed elsewhere does not establish that the same requirements apply here. The immediate need is a clear escalation and repeat-back.

Rosa: Foreperson / Dev: Safety coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is missing from the card?

Answer and explanation

A. The task-specific approved method, controls, and equipment requirements

The generic instruction does not supply the actual approved requirements for this task.

2. What is the affected task's current status?

Answer and explanation

B. Stopped under the local process

Rosa has stopped the affected activity pending the required qualified clarification.

3. What can experience on another site establish here?

Answer and explanation

D. It does not by itself establish the requirements for this task.

Different materials, tools, and conditions require the applicable task assessment rather than an assumed match.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

The phrase right protection supplies no usable task requirement. Clarification must address the applicable method and controls, not simply invent a list of items to wear. PPE is not a replacement for the wider control process.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
toolbox talk
A focused workplace briefing about a task or safety topic.deliver a toolbox talk
task briefing
Instructions and relevant information for a specific planned activity.clarify the task briefing
method statement
A document describing the planned method and relevant controls for work.consult the approved method statement
job hazard analysis (JHA)
A structured review of task steps, hazards, and controls.review the job hazard analysis
hazard identification
Recognition of conditions or activities that may cause harm.complete hazard identification
risk assessment
Evaluation of risks and the controls needed to manage them.review the risk assessment
hierarchy of controls
An ordered approach favoring more effective risk controls over reliance on individual behavior.apply the hierarchy of controls
engineering control
A physical or technical measure that reduces exposure to a hazard.verify engineering controls
administrative control
A work-practice or organizational measure intended to reduce risk.communicate administrative controls
personal protective equipment (PPE)
Equipment worn to help protect a person from specified hazards.specify task-appropriate PPE
exposure
Contact with or presence in conditions that may cause harm.assess worker exposure
dust extraction
Equipment or arrangements used to capture dust near its source.review dust extraction requirements
noise assessment
Evaluation of noise conditions and potential exposure.obtain a noise assessment
safety data sheet (SDS)
A document communicating information about a chemical product and its hazards.consult the safety data sheet
equipment guarding
Protective arrangements restricting access to hazardous equipment parts.verify equipment guarding
isolation
Separation from an energy source or hazard under the applicable process.follow the isolation procedure
exclusion zone
An area with access restricted for a specified safety reason.identify the exclusion zone
task competence
The knowledge and ability required to perform a particular task.verify task competence
site induction
Initial orientation to the requirements of a particular site.complete the site induction
repeat-back
Restating a message to confirm its accurate receipt and understanding.request a repeat-back
stop-work instruction
A direction to halt a specified activity under the relevant process.communicate a stop-work instruction
restart condition
A requirement that must be satisfied before work resumes.state the restart conditions
briefing record
Documentation of the briefing and relevant participation or acknowledgement.maintain the briefing record
near miss
An event that could have caused harm but did not produce the relevant loss.report a near miss

A signed attendance record does not demonstrate that each person understood the instruction. Use a repeat-back of the actual task status and next requirement. This dialogue does not supply a technical cutting method or authorize a restart.

C Notice the language

Repeat-back checks the message, not intelligence

Experienced workers can interpret the same vague phrase differently. Ask someone to restate the task status and next requirement as a check on the briefing itself. Do not ask for a yes merely to complete an attendance record.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Name the affected activity
The panel-cutting task in Zone B is stopped.
Identify the gap
The card does not specify the approved requirements.
Ask for the applicable document
Which approved method statement covers this task?
Request qualified review
Please obtain the task-specific assessment and controls.
Avoid equipment guessing
I will not select protective equipment from a generic phrase.
Broaden the control question
We need the full control arrangement, not PPE alone.
Distinguish similar work
Experience elsewhere does not confirm the requirements here.
State the restart boundary
The required clarification and authorization are still pending.
Check the message
Please repeat the task status and next requirement.
Correct a misunderstanding
Attending the talk does not mean the task is released.
Preserve responsibility
Dev will coordinate the qualified review.
Avoid an implied shortcut
Schedule pressure does not complete the missing assessment.
Record the current status
The briefing remains incomplete for this task.
Confirm the location
This concern applies to the planned activity in Zone B.
Make the next briefing specific
Identify the task, approved controls, and responsible supervisor.
Close the loop
Communicate an authorized status change explicitly before resuming.
6 language and precision notes
Right
Too vague unless the actual task requirement is identified.
Covers this task
Tests applicability, not merely the existence of a document.
Still pending
Keeps an unresolved condition visible during handover.
Repeat
Requests the message back, not a new safety plan from the learner.
Attendance versus understanding
A record of presence is not proof of comprehension.
Authorized status change
Prevents silence or movement on site from being mistaken for release.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which clarification is most useful?

Answer and explanation

A. Which approved method and controls apply to cutting these panels in Zone B?

The question identifies the task and asks for applicable approved requirements rather than a guess.

2. Which statement about controls is accurate?

Answer and explanation

C. PPE requirements belong within the applicable wider control arrangement.

Protective equipment does not replace assessing and applying the relevant task controls.

3. Which repeat-back matches the facts?

Answer and explanation

C. The task is stopped; qualified clarification and restart authorization are pending.

Receipt or attendance does not satisfy the missing clarification and authorization requirements.

4. What should Rosa avoid?

Answer and explanation

C. Inventing equipment requirements to complete the card

The case requires qualified task-specific clarification, not improvised protective-equipment selection.

  17. Rosa

  18. Dev
    The 9 helps frame that distinction. Qualified personnel must establish the actual measures; this exchange is about obtaining and communicating those requirements accurately.
  19. Rosa
    My immediate message is clear: Zone B panel cutting is stopped, the generic instruction is insufficient, and you are coordinating the task-specific review.
  20. Dev
    Correct. The next 10 needs that approved information and a check of understanding. We will not treat attendance, experience, or my acknowledgement as permission to resume.

Answers and explanations
  1. task briefing Turn 2. The task briefing needs applicable requirements; the generic phrase does not identify the method or controls.
  2. risk assessment Turn 4. The risk assessment addresses this task's conditions and controls; experience elsewhere does not establish its findings.
  3. Personal protective equipment Turn 6. Personal protective equipment protects against specified hazards but does not replace the wider task-control arrangement.
  4. method statement Turn 8. The method statement describes the planned method and controls; a similar title does not establish task applicability.
  5. briefing record Turn 10. A briefing record documents participation or the briefing; attendance alone establishes neither understanding nor authorization.
  6. repeat-back Turn 12. A repeat-back checks the listener's understanding of the task status and next requirement, beyond a vague acknowledgement.
  7. stop-work instruction Turn 14. The stop-work instruction identifies the halted activity and remains effective while clarification and authorization are pending.
  8. restart conditions Turn 16. Restart conditions must be satisfied before resumption; schedule pressure or uncertain timing does not remove them.
  9. hierarchy of controls Turn 18. The hierarchy of controls orders risk-control approaches; it does not treat PPE as the sole or preferred answer.
  10. toolbox talk Turn 20. The toolbox talk communicates the established task requirements; attendance does not replace the required restart authorization.
Read the complete conversation

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which task and approved protection?

Fictional toolbox-talk language exercise: an instruction says use the right protection but identifies neither the task nor its approved requirements; no actual safety method is supplied.

  1. Site trainee: The toolbox talk says use the right protection. Could you clarify which task that instruction covers?
  2. Safety coordinator: The task isn't identified in this excerpt, so we need the approved task-specific information.
  3. Site trainee: Can I just name familiar PPE so the rest of the group has an answer?
  4. Safety coordinator: No. Guessing equipment doesn't establish the approved protection for work we haven't even identified.
  5. Site trainee: Who should clarify the task and applicable requirements before this briefing exercise continues?
  6. Safety coordinator: We'll ask the authorized task supervisor for the current approved briefing materials and explanation.
  7. Site trainee: Should we also confirm whether the reference to fall protection belongs to this task?
  8. Safety coordinator: Yes. Ask about the exact reference without treating a general term as a complete instruction.
  9. Site trainee: I'll say the task and requirements remain unclear, rather than claim the briefing is complete.
  10. Safety coordinator: Good. In actual work, the applicable safety and stop-work procedures govern the response to uncertainty.
A protection abbreviation without an explanation

Separate fictional induction exercise: a briefing card contains an unexplained PPE abbreviation; trainees have not been given the task-specific approved instructions.

  1. Induction trainer: The briefing card uses an abbreviation that several trainees couldn't explain during the discussion.
  2. Site supervisor: Which abbreviation is unclear, and does the card identify the task it relates to?
  3. Induction trainer: It appears in the PPE section, but the card doesn't link it to a defined task.
  4. Site supervisor: Then we need both the meaning and the applicable task reference clarified by the authorized source.
  5. Induction trainer: Would expanding the letters alone be enough to mark the toolbox talk as understood?
  6. Site supervisor: No. Recognizing words isn't the same as understanding the approved requirement for a particular activity.
  7. Induction trainer: I'll ask trainees to identify what remains unclear without inviting them to invent a safety method.
  8. Site supervisor: Good. I'll obtain the current briefing material and arrange the appropriate task-specific explanation.
  9. Induction trainer: Afterward, we can ask them to repeat the clarification in their own words for language practice.
  10. Site supervisor: Yes, while making clear that this exercise doesn't authorize work or replace qualified safety instruction.
A changed task needs a clarified briefing

Separate fictional communication exercise: a supervisor's message changes the named work area but repeats an earlier generic protection instruction; actual work follows authorized procedures.

  1. Work coordinator: Your message names a different area, but repeats the earlier protection wording. Is the task unchanged?
  2. Site supervisor: I haven't confirmed that in the message. Thank you for identifying the ambiguity before the briefing.
  3. Work coordinator: Could you clarify the exact task and whether the approved briefing applies to this area?
  4. Site supervisor: I'll check with the responsible safety and task leads rather than assume the earlier instruction transfers.
  5. Work coordinator: Someone suggested saying same PPE as before to keep the toolbox talk short today.
  6. Site supervisor: That would hide the question we need answered. Brevity shouldn't replace the applicable approved information.
  7. Work coordinator: I'll keep the area change and outstanding clarification visible in the coordination note.
  8. Site supervisor: Please do. The note should request clarification, not offer instructions on fall protection or equipment use.
  9. Work coordinator: Once the authorized clarification arrives, I'll ask the receiving team to repeat the relevant status.
  10. Site supervisor: Agreed. Until then, actual activity remains governed by the local safety and stop-work process.

Language workshop: The briefing says use the right protection
Communication goal: Request task-specific, approved safety information and confirm understanding without inventing a work method.
Language guidance: The phrase right protection supplies no usable task requirement. Clarification must address the applicable method and controls, not simply invent a list of items to wear. PPE is not a replacement for the wider control process.

Vocabulary: toolbox talk: A focused workplace briefing about a task or safety topic. / task briefing: Instructions and relevant information for a specific planned activity. / method statement: A document describing the planned method and relevant controls for work. / job hazard analysis (JHA): A structured review of task steps, hazards, and controls. / hazard identification: Recognition of conditions or activities that may cause harm. / risk assessment: Evaluation of risks and the controls needed to manage them. / hierarchy of controls: An ordered approach favoring more effective risk controls over reliance on individual behavior. / engineering control: A physical or technical measure that reduces exposure to a hazard. / administrative control: A work-practice or organizational measure intended to reduce risk. / personal protective equipment (PPE): Equipment worn to help protect a person from specified hazards. / exposure: Contact with or presence in conditions that may cause harm. / dust extraction: Equipment or arrangements used to capture dust near its source. / noise assessment: Evaluation of noise conditions and potential exposure. / safety data sheet (SDS): A document communicating information about a chemical product and its hazards. / equipment guarding: Protective arrangements restricting access to hazardous equipment parts. / isolation: Separation from an energy source or hazard under the applicable process. / exclusion zone: An area with access restricted for a specified safety reason. / task competence: The knowledge and ability required to perform a particular task. / site induction: Initial orientation to the requirements of a particular site. / repeat-back: Restating a message to confirm its accurate receipt and understanding. / stop-work instruction: A direction to halt a specified activity under the relevant process. / restart condition: A requirement that must be satisfied before work resumes. / briefing record:
